## Quellen zu Customer Satisfaction (Agentensuche, 12. Oktober 2026)
Deep-Dive-Suche mit acht parallelen Rechercheagenten (drei Consensus-Blöcke zu Konzept und Messung, finanziellen und nicht-finanziellen Outcomes; Bücher und Reports; Websites, Blogs und Video-Tutorials; Lehrfälle und Praxisbeispiele; Software und Daten; People und Communities), Suche auf Englisch und Deutsch. 465 Fundstellen nach Dublettenbereinigung. Schwerpunkte: Messung der Kundenzufriedenheit (Importance-Performance-Analyse und Varianten wie Revised IPA, Penalty-Reward-Kontrastanalyse und asymmetrische IPA, Kano bzw. Drei-Faktoren-Theorie, SERVQUAL/SERVPERF, ACSI/EPSI, CSAT/NPS/CES) sowie finanzielle Outcomes (ROA, Cashflow, Tobin's q, Aktienrenditen, Kapitalkosten, Preisprämie) und nicht-finanzielle Outcomes (Loyalität, Weiterempfehlung und eWOM, Beschwerden und Service Recovery, Mitarbeiterzufriedenheit, Engagement). Ergänzt die Seite 'Customer Loyalty, Churn and Share of Wallet (Service-Profit Chain)'; Überschneidungen bei Satisfaction-Profit-Studien sind hier aus Zufriedenheitsperspektive kommentiert. Hinweise: drei häufig zitierte Effektgrößen (Anderson, Fornell & Lehmann 1994; Anderson, Fornell & Mazvancheryl 2004; Gruca & Rego 2005) sind in der Notiz als 'vor dem Zitieren prüfen' markiert; ACSI- und Verlagsseiten blockieren automatische Abrufe (403), solche Zeilen behalten 'in Suchergebnis bestätigt'; WU-Lizenzen für Qualtrics, SmartPLS und Unipark ungeklärt. Status: 'Link geprüft' = Seite abgerufen, 'in Suchergebnis bestätigt' = nur über Suchtreffer bestätigt, 'Link ungeprüft' = vor Verwendung prüfen. Maschinenlesbare Quelle: GitHub topics/Customer Satisfaction/rows.json.

<td>The definitive theory text. The introduction ('What is satisfaction?') and Part 1 cover attribute performance, expectations and the expectancy-disconfirmation model. <tr>
<td>Journal Articles</td>
<td>A Cognitive Model of the Antecedents and Consequences of Satisfaction Decisions</td>
<td>Richard L. Oliver, Journal of Marketing Research 17(4), 460-469, 1980, article</td>
<td>[https://doi.org/10.1177/002224378001700405](https://doi.org/10.1177/002224378001700405)</td>
<td>The founding statement of the expectation-disconfirmation paradigm: satisfaction is modelled as a function of prior expectations plus (dis)confirmation, and satisfaction in turn shifts post-use attitude and purchase intention. Two-stage field study with users and non-users of a flu inoculation, so students can see the pre/post measurement design that every later CS model inherits. Short (10 pages); the path diagram is the one to put on a slide. Caveat: the data context (vaccination) is far from typical marketing cases; pair with a commercial example.</td>
<td>Core reading for the session on the concept of satisfaction; draw the expectations -\> disconfirmation -\> satisfaction -\> intention chain in class. Access: paid (SAGE), WU-Lizenz prüfen.</td>
<td>neu; in Suchergebnis bestätigt</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Journal Articles</td>
<td>An Investigation into the Determinants of Customer Satisfaction</td>
<td>Gilbert A. Churchill Jr. and Carol Surprenant, Journal of Marketing Research 19(4), 491-504, 1982, article</td>
<td>[https://doi.org/10.1177/002224378201900410](https://doi.org/10.1177/002224378201900410)</td>
<td>Classic 3 x 3 factorial experiment (expectation level x performance level) for a nondurable (plant) and a durable (video disc player). For the nondurable the full disconfirmation model holds; for the durable, satisfaction is driven by performance alone and disconfirmation has no effect. This is the best teaching evidence that performance-only measures (later SERVPERF, ACSI 'perceived quality') can be enough. The comparison table of the two product models works well as a discussion slide.</td>
<td>Core or background reading next to Oliver (1980); use for a debate 'Do we need to measure expectations at all?'. Access: paid (SAGE), WU-Lizenz prüfen.</td>
<td>neu; in Suchergebnis bestätigt</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Journal Articles</td>
<td>Defining Consumer Satisfaction</td>
<td>Joan L. Giese and Joseph A. Cote, Academy of Marketing Science Review 2000(1), 2000, article (online journal)</td>
<td>[https://www.proquest.com/docview/200857197](https://www.proquest.com/docview/200857197)</td>
<td>Systematic review of 20 years of satisfaction definitions plus consumer focus groups; concludes that satisfaction is (1) an affective response of varying intensity, (2) focused on a specific object (product, purchase, consumption), (3) occurring at a particular time. Their definitional framework (response / focus / timing) is the cleanest way to show students why survey items must name the object and the time point (transaction-specific vs cumulative). Note: AMS Review was an online journal without DOIs; author-hosted copies circulate but were not verified.</td>
<td>Background reading for defining the construct; good basis for a seminar exercise where students critique real CSAT survey items against the three components. Access: via ProQuest (login), WU-Lizenz prüfen.</td>
<td>neu; Link geprüft</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Journal Articles</td>
<td>Rediscovering Satisfaction</td>
<td>Susan Fournier and David Glen Mick, Journal of Marketing 63(4), 5-23, 1999, article</td>
<td>[https://doi.org/10.1177/002224299906300403](https://doi.org/10.1177/002224299906300403)</td>
<td>Longitudinal, phenomenological (interview-based) study of households adopting technology products. Shows that the comparison-standard (disconfirmation) model fits some consumers but is irrelevant for others; satisfaction emerges as a dynamic, social, emotional process (e.g., 'satisfaction-as-resignation', 'satisfaction-as-delight'). Useful counterweight to purely survey-based CS measurement and to explain why single-item CSAT scores lose meaning.</td>
<td>Discussion reading in the concept session; contrast with Oliver (1980) and ask what a survey would miss. Access: paid (SAGE), WU-Lizenz prüfen.</td>
<td>neu; in Suchergebnis bestätigt</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Journal Articles</td>
<td>Kundenzufriedenheit</td>
<td>Bernd Stauss, Marketing ZFP 21(1), 5-24, 1999, article (German)</td>
<td>[https://doi.org/10.15358/0344-1369-1999-1-5](https://doi.org/10.15358/0344-1369-1999-1-5)</td>
<td>German-language overview article on the concept and measurement of customer satisfaction by one of the leading German services-marketing scholars (Eichstätt-Ingolstadt). Discusses the C/D paradigm and approaches to measuring satisfaction in German terminology (Kundenzufriedenheit, Zufriedenheitsmessung); Stauss is known for the distinction between attribute-based and event-based (incident) measurement, but the full text was not checked for this row. One of the few peer-reviewed German sources for this block.</td>
<td>Background reading in German for students who prefer German literature or write theses in German. Access: paid (Marketing ZFP / Beck eLibrary), WU-Lizenz prüfen.</td>
<td>neu; in Suchergebnis bestätigt</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Journal Articles</td>
<td>Customer Satisfaction: A Meta-Analysis of the Empirical Evidence</td>
<td>David M. Szymanski and David H. Henard, Journal of the Academy of Marketing Science 29(1), 16-35, 2001, article</td>
<td>[https://doi.org/10.1177/0092070301291002](https://doi.org/10.1177/0092070301291002)</td>
<td>Meta-analysis of the satisfaction literature: equity and disconfirmation have the strongest average correlations with satisfaction; expectations and performance matter less consistently, and measurement/method choices (e.g., scale type, single vs multi-item measures) moderate effect sizes. The summary table of average correlations per antecedent is ideal for a slide ranking the drivers of satisfaction.</td>
<td>Core reading for 'antecedents of satisfaction'; use the correlation table to test students' intuitions before revealing results. Access: paid (Springer), WU-Lizenz prüfen.</td>
<td>neu; Link geprüft</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Journal Articles</td>
<td>Expectancy-disconfirmation and consumer satisfaction: A meta-analysis</td>
<td>Tom Schiebler, Nick Lee and Felix C. Brodbeck, Journal of the Academy of Marketing Science 54(1), 91-112, 2026 (online first January 2025), article</td>
<td>[https://doi.org/10.1007/s11747-024-01078-x](https://doi.org/10.1007/s11747-024-01078-x)</td>
<td>Up-to-date meta-analytic path analysis of 150 records (N = 58,597): expectations correlate positively with satisfaction (r = .29), there is no evidence for contrast effects, and disconfirmation is a strong mediator. Effects are stronger for predictive (vs normative) expectations, for services, and in cross-sectional designs; an unusual downward publication bias is reported. Shows students that the 40-year-old paradigm still holds and how design choices shape findings.</td>
<td>Core reading to update Oliver (1980); good for a methods discussion on cross-sectional vs experimental evidence. Access: open access (Springer, CC BY).</td>
<td>neu; Link geprüft</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Journal Articles</td>
<td>Customer satisfaction: A multi-level framework</td>
<td>Vikas Mittal, Sonam Singh and Ashwin Malshe, Journal of the Academy of Marketing Science, online first, 2026, article</td>
<td>[https://doi.org/10.1007/s11747-026-01175-z](https://doi.org/10.1007/s11747-026-01175-z)</td>
<td>Very recent synthesis: defines CSAT as a post-consumption judgment of whether customers receive the promised value and separates it from service quality, experience, engagement, loyalty and value. Shows strong convergent validity of commonly used CSAT scales and uses more than 285 million ratings across 1,126 brands to link CSAT with mindset metrics, accounting and stock-market outcomes. Bridges both course emphases (measurement and outcomes) in one paper.</td>
<td>Core or capstone reading; use its construct-distinction section for the definitions lecture and its outcome evidence as a bridge to the outcomes session. Access: open access (Springer).</td>
<td>neu; Link geprüft</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Journal Articles</td>
<td>A framework for comparing customer satisfaction across individuals and product categories</td>
<td>Michael D. Johnson and Claes Fornell, Journal of Economic Psychology 12(2), 267-286, 1991, article</td>
<td>[https://doi.org/10.1016/0167-4870%2891%2990016-m](https://doi.org/10.1016/0167-4870%2891%2990016-m)</td>
<td>Conceptual foundation of cumulative satisfaction: satisfaction as an overall evaluation built up over the total consumption experience, which makes it comparable across people, firms and categories. This is the theoretical basis for national barometers (Sweden, ACSI, ECSI) and the reason they use cumulative rather than transaction-specific items. A repository copy is openly available in the University of Michigan Deep Blue repository.</td>
<td>Background reading for the cumulative vs transaction-specific distinction; assign before the ACSI/ECSI session. Access: paid (Elsevier) but open repository copy via Deep Blue (hdl.handle.net/2027.42/29302).</td>
<td>neu; in Suchergebnis bestätigt</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Journal Articles</td>
<td>Rational and Adaptive Performance Expectations in a Customer Satisfaction Framework</td>
<td>Michael D. Johnson, Eugene W. Anderson and Claes Fornell, Journal of Consumer Research 21(4), 695-707, 1995, article</td>
<td>[https://doi.org/10.1086/209428](https://doi.org/10.1086/209428)</td>
<td>Market-level model using Swedish Customer Satisfaction Barometer data: satisfaction is treated as a cumulative construct carried over from period to period, while market expectations are largely rational but adapt to recent performance. Useful to show what 'cumulative satisfaction' means empirically (lagged satisfaction as a predictor) and why expectations in index models differ from Oliver's individual-level expectations.</td>
<td>Advanced background reading (Master level) on cumulative satisfaction. Access: paid (Oxford/JCR), WU-Lizenz prüfen.</td>
<td>neu; in Suchergebnis bestätigt</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Journal Articles</td>
<td>The Antecedents and Consequences of Customer Satisfaction for Firms</td>
<td>Eugene W. Anderson and Mary W. Sullivan, Marketing Science 12(2), 125-143, 1993, article</td>
<td>[https://doi.org/10.1287/mksc.12.2.125](https://doi.org/10.1287/mksc.12.2.125)</td>
<td>Field test of the disconfirmation model on 22,300 Swedish customers (1989-1990). Key findings for class: satisfaction is best explained by perceived quality and disconfirmation, expectations have no direct effect, and negative disconfirmation hurts satisfaction and repurchase intention more than positive disconfirmation helps (early evidence of asymmetry). Links concept and outcomes.</td>
<td>Core reading linking the C/D paradigm to the asymmetry topic; use the asymmetric disconfirmation finding as a lead-in to Kano/three-factor theory. Access: paid (INFORMS), WU-Lizenz prüfen.</td>
<td>neu; in Suchergebnis bestätigt</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Journal Articles</td>
<td>A National Customer Satisfaction Barometer: The Swedish Experience</td>
<td>Claes Fornell, Journal of Marketing 56(1), 6-21, 1992, article</td>
<td>[https://doi.org/10.1177/002224299205600103](https://doi.org/10.1177/002224299205600103)</td>
<td>Describes the first national satisfaction index (Swedish Customer Satisfaction Barometer, SCSB, 1989-): cumulative satisfaction measured across 30+ industries and 100+ firms, estimated with a latent-variable (PLS) model. Key teaching points: satisfaction is lower where supply is homogeneous but demand heterogeneous, and higher in industries that depend on repeat business than in captive markets. Introduces the logic of satisfaction as a macro 'quality of output' indicator complementing productivity.</td>
<td>Core reading for the national-index session; follow with Fornell et al. (1996) on ACSI. Access: paid (SAGE/JSTOR), WU-Lizenz prüfen.</td>
<td>neu; in Suchergebnis bestätigt</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Journal Articles</td>
<td>The American Customer Satisfaction Index: Nature, Purpose, and Findings</td>
<td>Claes Fornell, Michael D. Johnson, Eugene W. Anderson, Jaesung Cha and Barbara Everitt Bryant, Journal of Marketing 60(4), 7-18, 1996, article</td>
<td>[https://doi.org/10.1177/002224299606000403](https://doi.org/10.1177/002224299606000403)</td>
<td>The reference description of the ACSI model (expectations, perceived quality, perceived value -\> satisfaction -\> complaints, loyalty), survey design and PLS estimation. Findings to show students: satisfaction is higher for goods than services and lowest for government agencies; customization matters more than reliability; satisfaction is more quality- than price-driven. The ACSI path model figure is the standard slide for index-based measurement.</td>
<td>Core reading; reproduce the ACSI path model in class and compare with current scores on theacsi.org. Access: paid (SAGE), WU-Lizenz prüfen.</td>
<td>neu; in Suchergebnis bestätigt</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Journal Articles</td>
<td>The evolution and future of national customer satisfaction index models</td>
<td>Michael D. Johnson, Anders Gustafsson, Tor Wallin Andreassen, Line Lervik and Jaesung Cha, Journal of Economic Psychology 22(2), 217-245, 2001, article</td>
<td>[https://doi.org/10.1016/s0167-4870%2801%2900030-7](https://doi.org/10.1016/s0167-4870%2801%2900030-7)</td>
<td>Compares the Swedish, US and emerging European index models and tests modifications with Norwegian Customer Satisfaction Barometer data: replacing expectations with corporate image, splitting loyalty from complaint handling, adding affective and calculative commitment. Good to explain why ECSI/EPSI add 'image' and drop some ACSI paths.</td>
<td>Background reading for comparing ACSI vs ECSI/EPSI model structures; students can sketch both models side by side. Access: paid (Elsevier), WU-Lizenz prüfen.</td>
<td>neu; in Suchergebnis bestätigt</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Journal Articles</td>
<td>Modelling customer satisfaction and loyalty on aggregate levels: Experience from the ECSI pilot study</td>
<td>Claes Cassel and Jan A. Eklöf, Total Quality Management 12(7-8), 834-841, 2001, article</td>
<td>[https://doi.org/10.1080/09544120100000004](https://doi.org/10.1080/09544120100000004)</td>
<td>Reports the European Customer Satisfaction Index (ECSI) pilot across 11 countries and industries such as telecoms, banking and supermarkets; shows that one common structural model (image, expectations, perceived product and service quality, value, satisfaction, loyalty) is robust enough for pan-European use, while 'perceived value' is the hardest construct to measure. Origin of today's EPSI Rating (incl. Austria).</td>
<td>Background reading for the European index model; connect to current EPSI country reports. Access: paid (Taylor & Francis), WU-Lizenz prüfen.</td>
<td>neu; in Suchergebnis bestätigt</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Journal Articles</td>

<td>Peter Hackl, Dieter Scharitzer and Reinhard Zuba, der markt 35(2), 86-94, 1996, article</td>
<td>[https://doi.org/10.1007/bf03032418](https://doi.org/10.1007/bf03032418)</td>
<td>Pilot study for an Austrian national satisfaction barometer modelled on the Swedish SCSB and ACSI, by authors from WU Vienna. Describes model, sampling and first results, i.e., a local, WU-specific example of how national CS indices were transferred to Austria. Short article (9 pages) published in the Austrian marketing journal der markt.</td>
<td>Background reading with local relevance for WU students; use as an anchor for discussing whether Austria has (or needs) a national index today. Access: paid (Springer), WU-Lizenz prüfen.</td>
<td>neu; Link geprüft</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Journal Articles</td>
<td>A Conceptual Model of Service Quality and Its Implications for Future Research</td>
<td>A. Parasuraman, Valarie A. Zeithaml and Leonard L. Berry, Journal of Marketing 49(4), 41-50, 1985, article</td>
<td>[https://doi.org/10.1177/002224298504900403](https://doi.org/10.1177/002224298504900403)</td>
<td>The GAP model of service quality from focus groups in four service businesses: perceived quality = gap 5 between expected and perceived service, caused by four provider gaps (knowledge, standards, delivery, communication), and ten original quality determinants later condensed into the five SERVQUAL dimensions. The gaps diagram is a must-have slide for distinguishing service quality from satisfaction.</td>
<td>Core reading for the service-quality measurement session; students map a real complaint to gaps 1-4. Access: paid (SAGE), WU-Lizenz prüfen.</td>
<td>neu; in Suchergebnis bestätigt</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Journal Articles</td>
<td>Measuring Service Quality: A Reexamination and Extension</td>
<td>J. Joseph Cronin Jr. and Steven A. Taylor, Journal of Marketing 56(3), 55-68, 1992, article</td>
<td>[https://doi.org/10.1177/002224299205600304](https://doi.org/10.1177/002224299205600304)</td>
<td>Introduces SERVPERF: the 22 SERVQUAL perception items without expectation scores outperform the perception-minus-expectation gap score in four industries (banking, pest control, dry cleaning, fast food). Also argues that service quality is an antecedent of satisfaction and that satisfaction has the stronger effect on purchase intentions. Read together with the 1994 SERVPERF vs SERVQUAL reply (JM 58(1), 125-131).</td>
<td>Core reading paired with Parasuraman et al. (1985/1988); debate the gap-score vs performance-only question. Access: paid (SAGE), WU-Lizenz prüfen.</td>
<td>neu; in Suchergebnis bestätigt</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Journal Articles</td>
<td>Importance-Performance Analysis</td>
<td>John A. Martilla and John C. James, Journal of Marketing 41(1), 77-79, 1977, article</td>
<td>[https://doi.org/10.1177/002224297704100112](https://doi.org/10.1177/002224297704100112)</td>
<td>The original IPA paper (only three pages): attribute importance and performance ratings are plotted in a 2 x 2 grid with four action quadrants ('Concentrate here', 'Keep up the good work', 'Low priority', 'Possible overkill'), illustrated with an automobile dealer's service department. The quadrant chart is the single most important figure for this course block; the paper also already discusses crosshair placement (means vs medians).</td>
<td>Core reading; reproduce the four-quadrant chart and have students build their own IPA in Excel with a short class survey. Access: paid (SAGE/JSTOR), WU-Lizenz prüfen.</td>
<td>neu; in Suchergebnis bestätigt</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Journal Articles</td>
<td>The Performance-Importance Response Function: Observations and Implications</td>
<td>Scott E. Sampson and Michael J. Showalter, The Service Industries Journal 19(3), 1-25, 1999, article</td>
<td>[https://doi.org/10.1080/02642069900000027](https://doi.org/10.1080/02642069900000027)</td>
<td>Shows empirically that attribute importance is not a fixed scalar but a function of performance: as performance on an attribute improves, its stated importance changes, which can reverse IPA improvement priorities. This is the key early critique of IPA's independence assumption and a precursor of the three-factor/Kano-based revisions.</td>
<td>Background reading for the 'IPA critique' session; ask students what happens to a 'Concentrate here' attribute after it has been fixed. Access: paid (Taylor & Francis), WU-Lizenz prüfen.</td>
<td>neu; in Suchergebnis bestätigt</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Journal Articles</td>
<td>Revisiting importance-performance analysis</td>
<td>Haemoon Oh, Tourism Management 22(6), 617-627, 2001, article</td>
<td>[https://doi.org/10.1016/s0261-5177%2801%2900036-x](https://doi.org/10.1016/s0261-5177%2801%2900036-x)</td>
<td>Critical review of IPA practice in hospitality and tourism with re-analysis of published data: questions the meaning of 'importance', the independence of importance and performance, the choice of crosshairs (scale mean vs data mean) and the use of an iso-rating (diagonal) line. A compact checklist of everything that can go wrong in an IPA, very useful before a student project.</td>
<td>Core methods reading before any student IPA project; turn Oh's issues into a grading checklist. Access: paid (Elsevier), WU-Lizenz prüfen.</td>
<td>neu; in Suchergebnis bestätigt</td>
</tr>
<tr>

<td>Kurt Matzler, Elmar Sauerwein and Kenneth A. Heischmidt, The Service Industries Journal 23(2), 112-129, 2003, article</td>
<td>[https://doi.org/10.1080/02642060412331300912](https://doi.org/10.1080/02642060412331300912)</td>
<td>Austrian-led (Innsbruck) study of bank customers showing that traditional IPA can be misleading when attributes are basic, performance or excitement factors, because their importance depends on their performance. Presents an analytical procedure (dummy-variable regression) to identify the factor type and shows how to extend the IPA grid accordingly. A direct bridge between IPA and the Kano/three-factor model.</td>
<td>Core reading for the IPA session (the 'revisited' paper the course asks for); compare its revised grid with Martilla and James (1977). Access: paid (Taylor & Francis), WU-Lizenz prüfen.</td>

<td>Kurt Matzler, Franz Bailom, Hans H. Hinterhuber, Birgit Renzl and Johann Pichler, Industrial Marketing Management 33(4), 271-277, 2004, article</td>

<td>B2B study (customers of an automotive supplier) by the Innsbruck group: dummy-variable regression confirms asymmetric effects of attribute performance on overall satisfaction, and the authors show that recommendations from a traditional IPA would be misleading. Short (7 pages) and the most-cited version of the 'IPA must be revised' argument; the side-by-side comparison of IPA quadrant vs factor classification is the slide to show.</td>

<td>[https://doi.org/10.1108/09564230210445078](https://doi.org/10.1108/09564230210445078)</td>
<td>Compares two ways of identifying basic, performance and excitement factors with data on satisfaction with a hospital's IT department: Vavra's importance grid (stated vs derived importance) and Brandt's penalty-reward contrast analysis. <td>Applies classic IPA and asymmetric impact-performance analysis (AIPA) to the same hotel data and shows that they produce different improvement priorities; argues AIPA is more efficient because it incorporates the asymmetric (penalty/reward) impact of attributes. <td>Applies PRCA and AIPA to 1,547,869 TripAdvisor multi-attribute ratings (9,596 hotels, 75 capital cities, travellers from 140 countries). Shows that asymmetric effects and attribute priorities vary by hotel class, traveller type and region. Demonstrates how three-factor analysis scales to big online-rating data.</td>

<td>Argues that the importance grid (stated vs derived importance) lacks a theoretical basis for Kano classification, but can be reinterpreted for prioritisation; proposes combining it with a modified PRCA ('determinance-asymmetry analysis'), illustrated with airline passenger satisfaction with airport services. <td>[https://doi.org/10.1108/09604521111100243](https://doi.org/10.1108/09604521111100243)</td>
<td>Evaluates five techniques for Kano classification (Kano questionnaire, penalty-reward contrast analysis, importance grid, qualitative methods such as CIT, direct classification) on validity and reliability. Key conclusion: only the Kano questionnaire and direct classification can classify attributes at the design stage; PRCA works for existing offers. The comparison table is an excellent method-selection guide.</td>

<td>[https://doi.org/10.1080/14783363.2013.791117](https://doi.org/10.1080/14783363.2013.791117)</td>
<td>Review of 147 papers (1984-2012) structuring Kano research into phases of emergence, exploration and explosion, and setting an agenda for an 'explanation' phase. Short overview of how the theory of attractive quality developed; open repository version available (Linköping DiVA).</td>

<td>[https://doi.org/10.1108/09564230510592289](https://doi.org/10.1108/09564230510592289)</td>
<td>Empirical test of the Kano 'life cycle' of quality attributes for an e-service: the service was 'indifferent' at launch, is 'attractive' for the market today, but early adopters already see it as one-dimensional or must-be. <td>[https://doi.org/10.1177/002224299806200104](https://doi.org/10.1177/002224299806200104)</td>
<td>Large surveys in health care (n = 4,517) and automobiles (n = 9,359 and 13,759) show that negative attribute performance has a larger impact on overall satisfaction and repurchase intention than positive performance, and that satisfaction shows diminishing sensitivity (prospect-theory pattern). <td>Core reading for nonlinear effects; plot the asymmetric response curve in class. Access: paid (SAGE), WU-Lizenz prüfen.</td>

<td>[https://doi.org/10.1177/109467050032001](https://doi.org/10.1177/109467050032001)</td>
<td>Explains why satisfaction programmes fail when managers assume linear, symmetric links: each link of the chain (attribute performance -\> satisfaction -\> retention -\> profit) is asymmetric and nonlinear. Uses examples from commercial satisfaction studies. Bridges measurement (attribute asymmetry) and outcomes (profit) in one readable paper; an open SSRN version is available.</td>
<td>Core reading connecting the measurement and the outcomes sessions. Access: paid (SAGE); free SSRN version (abstract 2345327).</td>

<td>[https://doi.org/10.1177/00222437211013781](https://doi.org/10.1177/00222437211013781)</td>
<td>Three studies on B2B satisfaction programmes: identifies eight strategic B2B attributes (e.g., product/service quality, pricing, safety, sales process, project management, CSR, communication, ongoing support), classifies them as satisfaction-balancing, -maintaining or -enhancing by their asymmetry, shows heterogeneity across industries and customer subgroups, and links satisfaction to sales. Rare B2B measurement evidence with financial outcomes.</td>

<td>Classic critical incident technique (CIT) study: 700 incidents from airline, hotel and restaurant customers sorted into three groups of employee behaviour (response to service delivery failures, response to customer needs and requests, unprompted and unsolicited actions). <td>Uses ACSI data (1994-2000) linked to COMPUSTAT/CRSP to compare six feedback metrics: average satisfaction scores predict future business performance best, Top-2-Box satisfaction also performs well, while net promoters and number of recommendations have little or no predictive value. <td>Practitioner article introducing the Customer Effort Score (CES): based on a study of more than 75,000 customers interacting with contact centres and self-service channels, reducing customer effort predicts loyalty better than exceeding expectations ('delight'). <td>Classic first test of the satisfaction-profitability link using the Swedish Customer Satisfaction Barometer (SCSB) and ROI. Satisfaction raises ROI with a lag. The paper's simulation is the source of the often-quoted figure that a sustained one-point-per-year rise in satisfaction over five years is worth several million USD in net income for a typical firm (about USD 7.5m, roughly 11% of current ROI; check the exact numbers in the forecasting section before quoting). <td>Swedish SCSB firms: satisfaction and productivity both raise ROI, but for services there is a trade-off (raising labour productivity tends to lower satisfaction where customization matters), so the joint pay-off is lower for services than for goods. <td>Accounting classic with three levels of evidence: customer level (telecom: satisfaction predicts retention, revenue and revenue growth), business-unit level (retail bank branches: satisfaction predicts future revenues/profits) and firm level (ACSI positively related to market value of equity beyond book values; small positive abnormal returns around ACSI releases). <td>Q1 2026 (released 12 May 2026): the national ACSI fell 0.3% to 76.7 (0-100 scale), about its 2013 level, and complaints reached a record high (+16%). <td>Sauro, J., MeasuringU, 2016, blog post</td>
<td>[https://measuringu.com/key-drivers/](https://measuringu.com/key-drivers/)</td>
<td>Concise primer: multiple regression with standardized betas (example: usability .55 versus stability .12 for likelihood to recommend), R², multicollinearity, logistic regression for binary outcomes, and Shapley as a more stable option. Includes a 2x2 importance-performance matrix (Fix / Leverage and Expand / Maintain / Low Gain) and the correlation-is-not-causation caveat.</td>
<td>Required pre-reading for the KDA/IPA lab. Free.</td>
<td>neu; Link geprüft</td>

<tr>
<td>Blogs</td>
<td>The One Number You Need to Grow (A Replication)</td>
<td>Sauro, J., MeasuringU, 2018, blog post</td>
<td>[https://measuringu.com/nps-replication/](https://measuringu.com/nps-replication/)</td>
<td>Re-tests Reichheld's NPS-growth claim with future rather than past growth. NPS explains about 38% of two-year growth on average (8-76% by industry) and about 30% of four-year growth, roughly half of what Reichheld reported. Finds a discrepancy for Korean auto insurance. Concludes NPS has reasonable but imprecise predictive validity.</td>
<td>Outcomes session: a worked example of how to test a metric's predictive validity. Free.</td>
<td>neu; Link geprüft</td>
</tr>
<tr>

<td>Sauro, J., MeasuringU, 2019, blog post</td>
<td>[https://measuringu.com/nps-discredited/](https://measuringu.com/nps-discredited/)</td>
<td>Balanced review of the academic critiques (Keiningham, Sharp, Rust): NPS is often not the single best predictor but is close, correlates highly with satisfaction and the ACSI, and many critiques have method problems. The most defensible critique is that satisfaction leads to recommendation (a causal chain).</td>
<td>Pair with the Sharp post for a structured pro/contra NPS debate. Free.</td>

### Cases for Teaching (18)
<table fit-page-width="true" header-row="true">
<tr>
<td>Kategorie</td>
<td>Titel / Name</td>
<td>Autor / Quelle / Firma</td>
<td>Link</td>
<td>Notiz</td>
<td>Verwendung im Kurs</td>
<td>Status</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Cases for Teaching</td>
<td>Xerox Corp.: The Customer Satisfaction Program</td>
<td>Melvyn A.J. Menezes, Jon D. Serbin; Harvard Business School, HBS case 591-055 (product page date 2002; there is also a rewritten (A) version, 594-109, built around the guarantee decision)</td>
<td>[https://store.hbr.org/product/xerox-corp-the-customer-satisfaction-program/591055](https://store.hbr.org/product/xerox-corp-the-customer-satisfaction-program/591055)</td>
<td>Set in August 1990. Xerox ranks customer satisfaction as its number-one priority, ahead of ROA and market share. The case describes the satisfaction measurement system (survey design, data analyses, follow-up) and ends with a decision on which of four researched satisfaction-guarantee types to introduce. 27 pages. A classic for linking a satisfaction measurement system to a strategic action (the guarantee) and to Baldrige-era TQM.</td>
<td>Full case discussion (90 min): evaluate the measurement system, then choose the guarantee. Works as the anchor case for the 'measuring satisfaction' session. Paid: HBP USD 11.95 list, about half price with a Premium Educator account; WU-Lizenz prüfen.</td>
<td>neu; Link geprüft</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Cases for Teaching</td>
<td>California Closets: Organizing the Customer Experience</td>
<td>Boris Groysberg, Annelena Lobb; Harvard Business School, HBS case 419-004, Nov 2018 (rev. May 2019); comes with a Video Short</td>
<td>[https://store.hbr.org/product/california-closets-organizing-the-customer-experience/419004](https://store.hbr.org/product/california-closets-organizing-the-customer-experience/419004)</td>
<td>A franchise network uses NPS survey data from all its locations to standardise and improve the customer experience. CEO Bill Barton wants to build on best practices and attract millennial customers, and the question is how NPS can support both goals. 39 pages. Good for showing NPS as an operating system (closed loop, comparing locations), not just a score.</td>
<td>Case discussion on running NPS in practice: location benchmarking, franchisee incentives, closing the loop. Show the video after the discussion. Paid: HBP USD 11.95 list; WU-Lizenz prüfen.</td>
<td>neu; Link geprüft</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Cases for Teaching</td>
<td>The Employee-Customer-Profit Chain at Sears</td>
<td>Anthony J. Rucci, Steven P. Kirn, Richard T. Quinn; Harvard Business Review, Jan-Feb 1998, pp. 82-97 (HBR reprint 98109; an OnPoint enhanced edition also exists)</td>
<td>[https://store.hbr.org/product/employee-customer-profit-chain-at-sears/98109](https://store.hbr.org/product/employee-customer-profit-chain-at-sears/98109)</td>
<td>More than 100 Sears executives spent three years building a model and 'Total Performance Indicators' that link management behaviour, employee attitudes, customer satisfaction and financial results. The headline estimate: a 5-unit gain in employee attitude drives a 1.3-unit gain in customer impression, which drives a 0.5 percentage-point gain in revenue growth. 16 pages. Caveat: the authors were Sears executives, and Sears' later decline makes a useful critical point.</td>
<td>Assign as the case reading for the service-profit-chain or outcomes session. Students reconstruct the causal chain and critique the method (causality, measurement). Paid: HBR USD 11.95; WU-Lizenz prüfen (also in Business Source/EBSCO via WU library).</td>
<td>neu; Link geprüft</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Cases for Teaching</td>
<td>Taco Bell, Inc. (1983-1994)</td>
<td>Lynda M. Applegate, Leonard A. Schlesinger, Dave DeLong; Harvard Business School case (May 1998, rev. Oct 2001); related: 'Taco Bell Corp.' (Schlesinger & Hallowell, 1991)</td>
<td>[https://www.hbs.edu/faculty/Pages/item.aspx?num=22136](https://www.hbs.edu/faculty/Pages/item.aspx?num=22136)</td>
<td>CEO John Martin's decade of service-model redesign. System sales grew from about USD 700 million in 1983 to USD 3.9 billion in 1994, with more than 10,000 outlets. The case is a classic illustration of the service-profit chain: value redesign, employee roles, and the satisfaction and retention links Heskett and Schlesinger used to build the framework.</td>
<td>Case discussion linking employee satisfaction and retention to customer satisfaction and growth. Pair with Heskett et al. (1994) 'Putting the Service-Profit Chain to Work'. Paid via HBP; WU-Lizenz prüfen.</td>
<td>neu; Link geprüft</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Cases for Teaching</td>
<td>The Ritz-Carlton: Using Information Systems to Better Serve the Customer</td>
<td>W. Earl Sasser Jr., Thomas O. Jones, Norman Klein; Harvard Business School case 395-064 (1994); a course copy and key takeaways are on MIT OpenCourseWare 15.778</td>
<td>[https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/15-778-management-of-supply-networks-for-products-and-services-summer-2004/resources/ritz_carlton/](https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/15-778-management-of-supply-networks-for-products-and-services-summer-2004/resources/ritz_carlton/)</td>
<td>Ritz-Carlton (a Baldrige Award winner) captures guest preferences through its Covia reservation system and its Encore guest-history system, backed by employee empowerment. A good case on turning satisfaction data into personalised service and 'delight' (Kano attractive attributes). The MIT OCW page gives free key takeaways. Authorship as listed by secondary sources, so check it on HBP.</td>
<td>Short case or warm-up on customer-information systems and delight. The free MIT OCW material suits preparation. Full case paid via HBP; WU-Lizenz prüfen.</td>
<td>neu; Link geprüft</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Cases for Teaching</td>
<td>JetBlue Airways: Valentine's Day 2007</td>
<td>Robert S. Huckman, Gary P. Pisano, Virginia Fuller; Harvard Business School case (2008), discussed in HBS Working Knowledge, 31 Mar 2008</td>
<td>[https://www.library.hbs.edu/working-knowledge/jetblues-valentines-day-crisis](https://www.library.hbs.edu/working-knowledge/jetblues-valentines-day-crisis)</td>
<td>An ice storm at JFK: only 17 of 156 scheduled departures left on day one, and more than 131,000 customers were affected. Passengers stuck on a plane for more than 3 hours got a full refund plus a free round-trip voucher. On 21 Feb 2007 JetBlue issued a Customer Bill of Rights with set compensation for delays and USD 1,000 for involuntary bumping. Students split on whether this detailed guarantee was needed given JetBlue's service reputation.</td>
<td>Case discussion on service failure, recovery and service guarantees (the satisfaction-recovery paradox). Combine with ACSI airline scores. Paid via HBP; the HBS Working Knowledge summary is free.</td>
<td>neu; Link geprüft</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Cases for Teaching</td>
<td>Enterprise Rent-A-Car: Measuring Service Quality (ESQi)</td>
<td>Harvard Business School case on Enterprise's ESQi (look up the current number on HBP); related free reading: Reichheld, The Ultimate Question (HBS Press 2006), ch. 4, excerpt in Chief Marketer, 14 Jun 2006</td>
<td>[https://hbsp.harvard.edu/search?N=&Ntt=Enterprise%20Rent-A-Car%20service%20quality](https://hbsp.harvard.edu/search?N=&Ntt=Enterprise%20Rent-A-Car%20service%20quality)</td>
<td>Opens with a customer (Kevin Kirkman) and asks whether branch managers' promotions should depend on their branch's ESQi score (the share of 'completely satisfied' customers in monthly telephone surveys). Data on the top-box measure: customers who were completely satisfied were about 3 times as likely to return. Unauthorised copies circulate online, so use only the licensed HBP version.</td>
<td>Case discussion: top-box vs mean scores, sample size per branch, gaming risk when scores drive promotions and pay. Paid via HBP; WU-Lizenz prüfen.</td>
<td>neu; Link geprüft</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Cases for Teaching</td>

<td>IMD (Cranfield/IMD), case IMD-5-0772, 2004 (©2011); distributed via IMD and The Case Centre</td>
<td>[https://imd.org/research-knowledge/marketing/case-studies/aggreko-a-measuring-customer-satisfaction-with-net-promoter-score](https://imd.org/research-knowledge/marketing/case-studies/aggreko-a-measuring-customer-satisfaction-with-net-promoter-score)</td>
<td>B2B power-rental firm: a new marketing head wants NPS as the firm's first coherent voice-of-the-customer metric, and some executives react coolly. Covers the pros and cons of NPS and the critical implementation issues. B2B context is less common in NPS teaching.</td>
<td>Case discussion on choosing and implementing a satisfaction/loyalty metric. Teach as a series with (B) and (C). Paid via IMD or The Case Centre; WU-Lizenz prüfen.</td>
<td>neu; in Suchergebnis bestätigt</td>
</tr>

<td>[https://doi.org/10.1177/027347538901100213](https://doi.org/10.1177/027347538901100213)</td>
<td>A teaching paper that applies an extended IPA to a real faculty-course evaluation and turns importance/performance results into improvement actions. It is a ready template for a classroom exercise in which students run an IPA on their own course.</td>

<td>[https://doi.org/10.1080/08841241.2010.526356](https://doi.org/10.1080/08841241.2010.526356)</td>
<td>Kano questionnaires on 19 professor attributes, given to 63 postgraduate students in a services-marketing course, classify attributes as must-be, one-dimensional or excitement. Personality and rapport turn out to be key. The Kano questionnaire can be reused directly in class.</td>

<td>[https://doi.org/10.1080/08832320209599705](https://doi.org/10.1080/08832320209599705)</td>
<td>Course design based on the Kano model: students 'market' their coursework to the professor as customer. A two-year experiment with 357 students found progress on nationally normed learning objectives. Shows how Kano categories can be made tangible for students.</td>

<tr>
<td>Cases for Teaching</td>
<td>Austrian rail data mini-case: ÖBB vs. Deutsche Bahn - punctuality, satisfaction and NPS (built from public reports)</td>
<td>Instructor-built case from public reports: ÖBB-Personenverkehr AG Qualitätsbericht 2024 (published Oct 2025); DB Daten & Fakten 2023 / Integrierter Bericht 2024; Rechnungshof press release 27 Feb 2026</td>
<td>[https://personenverkehr.oebb.at/dam/jcr:207ee81e-8969-4f08-94d9-3e67a54462cc/Qualitaetsbericht2024.pdf](https://personenverkehr.oebb.at/dam/jcr:207ee81e-8969-4f08-94d9-3e67a54462cc/Qualitaetsbericht2024.pdf)</td>
<td>Free data: ÖBB 2024 punctuality was 93.6% overall, 78.2% long-distance and 94.3% regional. The report breaks down delay causes (e.g. neighbouring railways 7.3 points in long-distance traffic). ÖBB reports an NPS of 73.1 at ticket counters after retraining about 300 staff, and says satisfaction values were stable and within statistical bands. Older VCÖ-based grades were 2.08-2.14. DB long-distance satisfaction stood at school grade 2.7 (2022 and 2023) against a target of ≤2.0. Students can relate operations KPIs to satisfaction and discuss which metrics belong in management targets.</td>
<td>Group exercise (60-90 min) or term-paper case: build a KPI tree (punctuality → satisfaction → loyalty/ridership) and compare ÖBB and DB. Free; German-language sources.</td>
<td>neu; Link geprüft</td>
</tr>

<td>Cases for Teaching</td>
<td>Wiener Linien customer survey as an IPA mini-case (attribute satisfaction scores)</td>

<td>[https://industriemagazin.at/artikel/wiener-linien-abends-fuehlt-sich-jeder-vierte-fahrgast-nicht-sicher/](https://industriemagazin.at/artikel/wiener-linien-abends-fuehlt-sich-jeder-vierte-fahrgast-nicht-sicher/)</td>
<td>Published attribute performance: overall satisfaction 98% (above 96% every year since 2010); U-Bahn intervals 97%; network coverage 93%; price-performance 88%; tram intervals 79%; evening safety 76% (U-Bahn) and 81% (tram/bus). Importance is not published, so students collect importance ratings from Vienna residents (or classmates) and build an IPA grid. Caveat: the article is undated and the numbers are older; check for newer Wiener Linien press releases.</td>
<td>Local IPA exercise: students add importance data, plot the quadrants and discuss the near-ceiling 98% (top-box vs mean, scale use). Free.</td>
<td>neu; Link geprüft</td>

<tr>
<td>Praxisbeispiele</td>
<td>Enterprise Rent-A-Car ESQi: one top-box question, promotions tied to scores</td>
<td>Chief Marketer, 14 Jun 2006, excerpt adapted from Fred Reichheld, The Ultimate Question (HBS Press 2006); article</td>
<td>[https://chiefmarketer.com/the-enterprise-story-measuring-what-matters](https://chiefmarketer.com/the-enterprise-story-measuring-what-matters)</td>
<td>Since July 1994 Enterprise has asked one question: 'Overall, how satisfied were you with your recent car rental?' on a 5-point scale, starting by mail and later by telephone, with at least 25 responses per branch per month and a 3-month moving average. Customers giving the top score (5, 'completely satisfied') were about 3 times as likely to rent again. The top question explained 86% of the variation in referrals and repurchases. From 1996, branches had to be at or above the corporate ESQi average to win awards, and managers below average could not be promoted ('jacks or better').</td>
<td>Warm-up example for top-box measurement and for linking metrics to incentives (and the gaming risk). Free.</td>
<td>neu; Link geprüft</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Praxisbeispiele</td>
<td>Sears employee-customer-profit model: 5 → 1.3 → 0.5%</td>
<td>Rucci, Kirn & Quinn, Harvard Business Review, Jan-Feb 1998 (reprint 98109); article</td>
<td>[https://store.hbr.org/product/employee-customer-profit-chain-at-sears/98109](https://store.hbr.org/product/employee-customer-profit-chain-at-sears/98109)</td>
<td>Sears' Total Performance Indicators model: a 5-unit improvement in employee attitude drives a 1.3-unit improvement in customer impression, which drives a 0.5 percentage-point improvement in revenue growth. The model came out of the 1993-1995 turnaround after Sears' large loss in 1992. Caveat: the effect sizes come from the article's model (often quoted second-hand), and Sears' later decline is a good discussion point.</td>
<td>Lecture example for the satisfaction → financial outcomes chain; exam question on causal inference. Paid reprint (USD 11.95), also via WU library databases.</td>
<td>neu; Link geprüft</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Praxisbeispiele</td>
<td>ACSI 'Leaders' stock portfolio vs. S&P 500 (2006-2026)</td>
<td>American Customer Satisfaction Index (ACSI), 'ACSI scores as financial indicators' web page and press releases (2025-2026); company source</td>
<td>[https://theacsi.com/the-acsi-difference/acsi-scores-as-financial-indicators/](https://theacsi.com/the-acsi-difference/acsi-scores-as-financial-indicators/)</td>
<td>Jan 2006 to Mar 2026: the ACSI Leaders portfolio (25-35 stocks with higher ACSI scores than competitors) returned 2,340% cumulative, against 675% for the S&P 500 and 580% for the S&P 500 Equal Weight. Annualised: 17.1% vs 10.6% vs 9.9%. Through Jan 2025: 2,265% vs 605%. Caveats: published by ACSI itself; the result depends on the time window (12 months to Jul 2025: +16.5% vs +16.3%); dividends, costs and survivorship need discussion.</td>
<td>Lecture example for satisfaction → shareholder value, with a critical discussion of benchmark choice and self-reported performance. Free.</td>
<td>neu; Link geprüft</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Praxisbeispiele</td>
<td>Fornell et al. (2006): Customer satisfaction and stock prices - high returns, low risk</td>
<td>Claes Fornell, Sunil Mithas, Forrest V. Morgeson III, M.S. Krishnan; Journal of Marketing 70(1), 2006, DOI 10.1509/jmkg.70.1.003; academic article</td>
<td>[https://doi.org/10.1509/jmkg.70.1.003](https://doi.org/10.1509/jmkg.70.1.003)</td>
<td>Feb 1997 to May 2003: a portfolio of firms in the top 20% of ACSI (relative to competitors) and above the national average earned a cumulative 40% (excluding dividends and costs) against 13% for the S&P 500, with a portfolio beta of 0.78. A later JM study (2016) reports 518% vs 31% for 2000-2014. Critical follow-ups (e.g. Jacobson & Mizik 2009) question the anomaly.</td>
<td>Reading for the financial-outcomes session; pair with the critique for a debate format. WU-Lizenz (SAGE/AMA) prüfen.</td>
<td>neu; Link ungeprüft</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Praxisbeispiele</td>
<td>ACSI Travel Study 2025: airline satisfaction ranking (Southwest #1)</td>
<td>ACSI press release, 22 Apr 2025, 'Travel Study 2025'; secondary coverage TravelPulse</td>
<td>[https://theacsi.com/news-and-resources/press-releases/2025/04/22/press-release-travel-study-2025/](https://theacsi.com/news-and-resources/press-releases/2025/04/22/press-release-travel-study-2025/)</td>
<td>Southwest +3% to 80 (number one, displacing Alaska); Delta and JetBlue 77 (unchanged); Alaska -7% to 76; Spirit +3% to 69. Overall airline satisfaction fell in 2025. Students can compare scores with business models (low-cost vs legacy) and with load factor and profitability.</td>

<td>[https://www.mactech.com/2025/01/28/apple-store-satisfaction-drops-5-in-latest-acsi-retail-and-consumer-shopping-study/amp/](https://www.mactech.com/2025/01/28/apple-store-satisfaction-drops-5-in-latest-acsi-retail-and-consumer-shopping-study/amp/)</td>
<td>Amazon is the second-highest online retailer at 83, unchanged. In warehouse clubs, Costco scores 82 (unchanged) and Sam's Club leads at 85. Apple Store satisfaction fell 5%. Note: Costco gets different scores in different ACSI categories (79/81/82), which is a good lesson in checking which category a score belongs to.</td>

<tr>
<td>Praxisbeispiele</td>
<td>Costco membership renewal rate as a financial loyalty outcome</td>
<td>Costco FY2025 Q4 results, reported by Nasdaq/Zacks (Oct 2025); company filings</td>
<td>[https://www.nasdaq.com/articles/costcos-renewal-rate-slips-898-temporary-dip-or-new-normal](https://www.nasdaq.com/articles/costcos-renewal-rate-slips-898-temporary-dip-or-new-normal)</td>
<td>FY2025 year-end renewal rate: 92.3% in the US and Canada (down 40 bp from the prior quarter) and 89.8% worldwide; 81.0 million paid members and 145.2 million cardholders. Management attributes the dip to online sign-ups (e.g. a Groupon campaign) entering the base, which shows that retention depends on acquisition channel. Membership fees are a large share of operating income, so satisfaction translates directly into recurring revenue.</td>
<td>Example of a behavioural loyalty metric alongside ACSI (82). Discuss satisfaction vs retention and channel effects. Free.</td>
<td>neu; Link geprüft</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Praxisbeispiele</td>
<td>Subscription TV and ISPs: chronically low ACSI scores (Comcast Xfinity 68)</td>
<td>ACSI Telecommunications benchmarks 2024/2025; Statista summary; Telecompetitor</td>
<td>[https://theacsi.com/industries/interactive-media-subscription-entertainment/subscription-tv-service/](https://theacsi.com/industries/interactive-media-subscription-entertainment/subscription-tv-service/)</td>
<td>2025 subscription TV: Verizon Fios 78, Xfinity (Comcast) 68 (2024: 69), Optimum (Altice) and Mediacom 62. In 2019 subscription TV ranked last of the 46 ACSI industries. ISPs rose 4% to 71 in 2024, with fiber ahead (Xfinity Fiber 75). Useful for discussing low satisfaction in markets with switching barriers and limited competition (satisfaction ≠ loyalty).</td>
<td>Discussion: why do firms with low satisfaction keep their customers? (switching costs, monopoly). Free.</td>
<td>neu; Link geprüft</td>

<tr>
<td>Praxisbeispiele</td>
<td>Wells Fargo fake-accounts scandal: ACSI falls to last among national banks</td>
<td>ACSI Finance and Insurance Report, 14 Nov 2017 (press release); InsuranceNewsNet; Motley Fool (Apr 2017) for the J.D. Power comparison</td>
<td>[https://theacsi.com/news-and-resources/press-releases/2017/11/14/press-release-finance-and-insurance-2017/](https://theacsi.com/news-and-resources/press-releases/2017/11/14/press-release-finance-and-insurance-2017/)</td>
<td>About 3.5 million potentially unauthorised accounts (2009-2016); USD 185 million in regulatory fines plus a USD 142 million class-action settlement. Wells Fargo's ACSI fell 3% to 74, last among national banks (national bank average 78, Citibank 81); in 2016 it had scored 76. J.D. Power 2017 showed Wells Fargo improving in all 11 regions, so different instruments gave different pictures. The incentive system (sales targets) caused the scandal.</td>
<td>Case-like example on incentive-driven misconduct and reputation; compare ACSI with J.D. Power (instrument differences). Free.</td>
<td>neu; Link geprüft</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Praxisbeispiele</td>
<td>Volkswagen 'Dieselgate': ACSI drops while the industry rises (2016)</td>
<td>ACSI Automobile Report 2016; coverage Green Car Reports and Christian Science Monitor (26 Aug 2016)</td>
<td>[https://www.csmonitor.com/Business/In-Gear/2016/0826/Once-a-leader-Volkswagen-is-near-the-bottom-in-customer-satisfaction](https://www.csmonitor.com/Business/In-Gear/2016/0826/Once-a-leader-Volkswagen-is-near-the-bottom-in-customer-satisfaction)</td>
<td>In a year when the automobile industry's ACSI rose 3.8% to 82, VW fell 3% from 80 to 78, near the bottom of 24 brands (tied with Dodge and Jeep; Acura last at 76). Sister brand Audi rose from 78 to 83. Based on 3,776 surveys (Q2 2016). ACSI's director warned that the reputational damage could outlast the fines.</td>
<td>Example of a brand-level shock vs owner satisfaction (owners judge the product as well as the brand). Highly relevant for a German-speaking audience. Free.</td>
<td>neu; Link geprüft</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Praxisbeispiele</td>
<td>United Airlines 2017 passenger-dragging incident: ACSI 70 → 67 → 70</td>
<td>ACSI Travel Reports 2017-2019; TravelPulse; Morning Consult brand tracking (via WTTW 2018)</td>
<td>[https://www.travelpulse.com/news/airlines-airports/where-does-traveler-satisfaction-rank-in-2018](https://www.travelpulse.com/news/airlines-airports/where-does-traveler-satisfaction-rank-in-2018)</td>
<td>United scored 70 in the survey for Apr 2016 to Mar 2017 and 67 (-4%) for Apr 2017 to Mar 2018, below ultra-low-cost carriers Frontier and Spirit. In 2019 it recovered to 70. Morning Consult's brand rating fell 47 points after the incident. Caveat: the ACSI window only partly covers the period after the incident, and J.D. Power showed record industry satisfaction in 2017.</td>
<td>Discussion of brand perception vs experienced satisfaction, and of measurement timing. Free.</td>

<tr>
<td>Praxisbeispiele</td>
<td>Ryanair 'Always Getting Better': customer-service turnaround and profits</td>
<td>Marketing Week (2015); Ryanair RNS Q3 FY2015 results (2 Feb 2015); Insider.co.uk; company statements</td>
<td>[https://marketingweek.com/higher-profits-and-bookings-validate-customer-service-improvements-says-ryanair/](https://marketingweek.com/higher-profits-and-bookings-validate-customer-service-improvements-says-ryanair/)</td>
<td>After a 2013 profit warning and criticism of its service, Ryanair launched AGB (2014): lower fees, allocated seating, relaxed bag rules, a better website. H1 FY2015 profit +32% to €795m. Q3 profit €49m vs a €35m loss a year earlier, with load factor up from 82% to 88%. FY to Mar 2015 profit +66% to €867m on 90.6m passengers. Q1 FY2016 +152% to €197m. Caveats: company attribution; lower fuel costs and fares also contributed.</td>
<td>Discussion: do satisfaction improvements pay off for a low-cost carrier, and how would you isolate the effect? Free (Marketing Week may need registration).</td>
<td>neu; Link geprüft</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Praxisbeispiele</td>
<td>T-Mobile US 'Un-carrier': churn falls as service and value improve</td>
<td>T-Mobile press releases and earnings (Q4 2013, Q1 and Q2 2015); J.D. Power 2015 Wireless Customer Care Study; GeekWire 2014</td>
<td>[https://www.t-mobile.com/news/press/2015-q1-earnings](https://www.t-mobile.com/news/press/2015-q1-earnings)</td>
<td>Branded postpaid churn fell from 2.5% (Q4 2012) to 1.7% (Q4 2013) and 1.30% (Q1 2015, a record low). In July 2015 T-Mobile ranked highest among full-service carriers in J.D. Power customer care. Caveat: company-reported figures; the Un-carrier moves (no contracts, no overage fees) changed value perceptions as well as satisfaction.</td>
<td>Example of satisfaction/value strategy → churn → customer lifetime value. A CLV calculation exercise is possible. Free.</td>
<td>neu; in Suchergebnis bestätigt</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Praxisbeispiele</td>
<td>Apple cites '98-99% iPhone satisfaction' on earnings calls</td>
<td>Apple earnings calls (fiscal Q3 2018 onward), citing 451 Research; coverage 9to5Mac, Benzinga, Mac Observer; critique by PerfectRec (via PhoneArena)</td>
<td>[https://www.phonearena.com/news/Apples-claim-about-how-satisfied-iPhone-14-owners-are-is-statistically-impossible_id148992](https://www.phonearena.com/news/Apples-claim-about-how-satisfied-iPhone-14-owners-are-is-statistically-impossible_id148992)</td>
<td>Q3 2018: Tim Cook cited 98% satisfaction for the iPhone X; CFO Maestri cited 96% across all iPhones and 98% for iPhone 8/8 Plus/X combined. Later claims: 99% for iPhone 15 and iPhone 17. PerfectRec calls these 'statistically impossible' for a representative sample (self-selection of early adopters). A good example of satisfaction reported in investor communication and of ceiling effects.</td>
<td>Critical-thinking warm-up: who measured it, how, which sample, and why report it to investors? Free.</td>
<td>neu; in Suchergebnis bestätigt</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Praxisbeispiele</td>
<td>Charles Schwab: NPS-driven turnaround (2004-2008)</td>
<td>Bain & Company brief 'What it takes to win in customer experience'; Reichheld & Markey, The Ultimate Question 2.0 (2011); consultant source</td>
<td>[https://media.bain.com/Images/BB_What_it_takes_to_win_in_customer_experience.pdf](https://media.bain.com/Images/BB_What_it_takes_to_win_in_customer_experience.pdf)</td>
<td>Schwab struggled in 2004. Within about four years it was an industry leader again and its share price had roughly tripled. Management credits the Net Promoter System, and CEO Walt Bettinger calls it a way to simplify hard decisions. Caveat: Bain is NPS's originator and not neutral, and Schwab's recovery also involved price cuts and market effects.</td>
<td>Example of NPS as a management system; use alongside the Keiningham critique. Free.</td>
<td>neu; Link geprüft</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Praxisbeispiele</td>
<td>WSJ (2019): 'The Dubious Management Fad Sweeping Corporate America' - NPS in earnings calls</td>
<td>Khadeeja Safdar & Inti Pacheco, Wall Street Journal, May 2019; responses from Bain podcast and HBR 'Net Promoter 3.0' (2021)</td>
<td>[https://www.bain.com/insights/dubious-management-fad-no-but-room-for-improvement-podcast](https://www.bain.com/insights/dubious-management-fad-no-but-room-for-improvement-podcast)</td>
<td>50 S&P 500 companies mentioned NPS more than 150 times in 2018 earnings calls, and none reported a decline in their own score. NPS is self-reported and unaudited, often from a single in-house survey question. Bain responded that the headline was extreme but the criticism of misuse was fair. Good material for the session on criticising NPS and on firms scaling back its use.</td>
<td>Debate prompt: should NPS be reported to investors or tied to executive pay? WSJ is paywalled; the Bain response is free.</td>
<td>neu; Link geprüft</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Praxisbeispiele</td>
<td>Keiningham et al. (2007): NPS does not outperform ACSI-type measures in predicting growth</td>
<td>Timothy L. Keiningham, Bruce Cooil, Tor W. Andreassen, Lerzan Aksoy; Journal of Marketing 71(3), 2007, DOI 10.1509/jmkg.71.3.39; MSI/H. Paul Root Award 2007</td>
<td>[https://doi.org/10.1509/jmkg.71.3.39](https://doi.org/10.1509/jmkg.71.3.39)</td>
<td>Longitudinal data from 21 Norwegian firms with 15,500+ interviews (Norwegian Customer Satisfaction Barometer), compared with ACSI. Using industries Reichheld cites as NPS exemplars, the study fails to replicate the 'clear superiority' of NPS for predicting revenue growth. The evidence-based counterweight to the NPS examples.</td>
<td>Core reading for the NPS-critique session; pair with the Schwab or Allianz examples. WU-Lizenz (AMA/SAGE) prüfen.</td>
<td>neu; in Suchergebnis bestätigt</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Praxisbeispiele</td>
<td>Tying NPS to pay: Cigna executives and Reichheld's warning</td>
<td>CX Dive (2025) on NPS in compensation, citing Forrester; Bain/HBR 'Before you link pay to customer feedback: five essentials'</td>
<td>[https://www.customerexperiencedive.com/news/compensation-nps-scores-employee-executive-bonuses/746663/](https://www.customerexperiencedive.com/news/compensation-nps-scores-employee-executive-bonuses/746663/)</td>
<td>Cigna tied senior executive pay to NPS. According to Forrester, half of CX leaders say their firms reward employees for hitting NPS targets. NPS inventor Fred Reichheld calls pay-linking 'a fundamentally flawed idea that will destroy the business in 95% of the cases', unless it covers only senior executives and uses relative performance. Shows the gaming and Goodhart's-law risk.</td>
<td>Discussion: when should satisfaction metrics drive incentives? Link to the Enterprise and Vodafone examples. Free.</td>
<td>neu; in Suchergebnis bestätigt</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Praxisbeispiele</td>
<td>Vodafone Annual Report 2025: NPS leadership in 9 of 15 markets, NPS in executive remuneration</td>
<td>Vodafone Group Plc, Annual Report 2025 (FY ending Mar 2025), strategic report p. 11 and remuneration report; annual report</td>
<td>[https://reports.investors.vodafone.com/view/897876789/11/](https://reports.investors.vodafone.com/view/897876789/11/)</td>
<td>Leading or co-leading consumer NPS in 9 of 15 markets; 6% fewer 'deep detractors'. The UK has its lowest-ever share of detractors and ranks first in its market; Germany reports a 'step-change' in NPS after the customer-base crisis. The remuneration report uses NPS, revenue market share and churn as customer measures in the bonus, and performance was judged above the midpoint. Vodafone Business uses a benchmark NPS method.</td>
<td>Example of satisfaction metrics in annual reports and in pay; students read the KPI definitions in the report. Free.</td>
<td>neu; Link geprüft</td>
</tr>

<td>2024: 72% of Allianz businesses outperform their local market on NPS and 57% are 'loyalty leaders' (above the 2024 target). New ambition: at least 60% NPS loyalty leadership positions by 2027. In 2020, 79% were above market average, beating the 2021 target of 75% a year early. Management noted a slight NPS dip linked to strong premium increases (price vs satisfaction trade-off). Allianz also uses a 'Digital NPS'.</td>

<tr>

<td>Satisfaction and loyalty are measured with the TRI\*M index (Kantar/Infratest method, range -66 to +134). Group TRI\*M (excluding T-Mobile US) was 77.6 at the end of 2024, up from 76.2. NPS has been tracked as an additional KPI since 2021 and was stable or improved in all consumer segments (Germany, US, Europe). A German example of a proprietary index vs NPS.</td>

<td>NPS for the network airlines (Lufthansa, SWISS, Austrian Airlines, Brussels) fell from +62 (2020) to +27, against the group's own target and industry benchmark of +50. Eurowings reached 44 against a target of 45. NPS reportedly feeds into Executive Board bonuses and is reviewed monthly. Drivers: 2023 cancellations and baggage delays. Caveat: secondary source; check the figures in the annual report's NPS section.</td>

<td>About 300 ticket-counter staff at around 50 counters were retrained on a new system, and the result was documented as NPS 73.1. Customer surveys are run along the mobility chain (station and train) by independent institutes; satisfaction rose during the pandemic (lower load factors) and has returned to pre-pandemic ranges. 2024 punctuality (P5): long-distance 78.2%, regional 94.3%, overall 93.6%; quality targets were missed because of the 2024 flood. Earlier reports (2012-2017) gave overall satisfaction grades of 2.08-2.15.</td>

<td>Praxisbeispiele</td>
<td>Hospitals: HCAHPS patient experience determines part of Medicare payments</td>

<td>Medicare withholds 2% of hospitals' base operating DRG payments (1% at the FY2013 start, rising to 2% by 2017) and hospitals earn it back on a Total Performance Score. Debate: overweighting subjective satisfaction, and safety-net hospitals being disadvantaged.</td>

<td>J.D. Power awards: licensing satisfaction rankings for advertising</td>

<td>[https://jdpower.com/business/brand-licensing/](https://jdpower.com/business/brand-licensing/)</td>
<td>The licensing model started after a 1984 Super Bowl ad: Subaru advertised a second place unauthorised, then Toyota wanted to promote its first place and J.D. Power charged USD 350. Today only top performers in each category may license award use, and ad claims are reviewed. Ranked brands do not pay to take part, but they pay to promote a win. Rankings rest on numerical scores, not necessarily on statistical significance. Consumer Reports forbids marketing use of its results.</td>

<td>Austrian destination benchmarking linked to guest satisfaction (Tyrol)</td>

<td>Extends the Austrian government's destination-benchmarking indicator system (in place since 1987) with guest-satisfaction measures. Around 290 citations.</td>

<td>Praxisbeispiele</td>
<td>Rejuvenating IPA: European bank, 5,209 customers, regression-based importance</td>
<td>Alessandro Arbore, Bruno Busacca; Journal of Service Management 22(3), 2011, DOI 10.1108/09564231111136890</td>
<td>[https://doi.org/10.1108/09564231111136890](https://doi.org/10.1108/09564231111136890)</td>
<td>Uses survey data from 5,209 customers of a European bank to compare classic IPA with a revised IPA that uses regression coefficients (derived importance) and models non-linear (three-factor) effects. Shows that ignoring non-linearity can lead to wrong priorities. Relevant for bank cases such as Erste Bank and Raiffeisen.</td>
<td>Advanced reading for IPA methodology; replicate the logic with class data. WU-Lizenz (Emerald) prüfen.</td>
<td>neu; in Suchergebnis bestätigt</td>

<td>[https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jretconser.2025.104243](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jretconser.2025.104243)</td>
<td>A representative sample of 798 consumers classifies 25 shopping-app features (product finder, wish list, check-and-reserve, visual search, AR, etc.) as must-be, attractive, one-dimensional or indifferent. <td>Software</td>
<td>seminr: Building and Estimating Structural Equation Models</td>
<td>Ray, Danks, Calero Valdez et al., CRAN, v2.5.0, GPL-3, R package</td>
<td>[https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/seminr/index.html](https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/seminr/index.html)</td>
<td>PLS-SEM and CB-SEM with a domain-specific syntax; ships the ECSI 'mobi' mobile-phone dataset (250 respondents) and a vignette estimating the ECSI model (image, expectations, perceived quality, value, satisfaction, complaints, loyalty); bootstrapping, HTMT, PLSpredict, mediation; total effects usable for IPMA. Also used in Hair et al. 'PLS-SEM Using R' (open access, corporate reputation example).</td>
<td>Core lab: replicate ECSI on mobi in one session; students then extend the model with their own survey. Free.</td>
<td>neu; Link geprüft</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Software</td>

<td>[https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/seminr/vignettes/SEMinR.html](https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/seminr/vignettes/SEMinR.html)</td>
<td>Step-by-step code for measurement and structural model of the European Customer Satisfaction Index; shows how satisfaction mediates perceived quality/value effects on loyalty. GitHub repo: [https://github.com/sem-in-r/seminr](https://github.com/sem-in-r/seminr).</td>
<td>Ready-made lab handout; ask students to build an importance-performance map from total effects and rescaled latent variable scores. Free.</td>

<td>Classic PLS-PM package with the 'satisfaction' (ECSI, 250 obs) dataset, multigroup comparison and REBUS segmentation. Companion free e-book 'PLS Path Modeling with R' (Sanchez 2013) walks through the ECSI satisfaction model.</td>
